paraphrase is compared against the one produced from the non-rigorous

is either wording-driven, arbitrary model variation, or a mistake.

table in each model's section cross-tabulates the two independently.

#26 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $n$ be a natural number and let $\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\dots,\lambda_n$ be real numbers, and define $g(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_k\cos(k\theta)$ for $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$; prove that if $g(\theta)\ge -1$ for all $\theta\in\mathbb{R}$, then $\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_k\le n$.
   Lean       : theorem cosine_sum_le_degree (n : ℕ) (λ : Fin n → ℝ) (h : ∀ θ : ℝ, -1 ≤ ∑ k : Fin n, λ k * Real.cos ((↑(k.1 + 1) : ℝ) * θ)) : ∑ k : Fin n, λ k ≤ (n : ℝ) := by sorry
   ERROR [other] The rigorous branch does not parse: it uses 'λ' as a binder name, and λ is reserved in Lean 4 as notation for 'fun' (\"unexpected token 'λ'; expected '_' or identifier\").
     why  : A statement that does not parse asserts nothing, so the pair is not comparable as mathematics until the name is changed. The intended content is identical to the prose branch's and is correct — this is the classical Fejér-type bound: g(θ) ≥ -1 for all θ forces Σλₖ ≤ n.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: The rigorous paraphrase prints the coefficients as '$\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\dots,\lambda_n$' and this branch used the printed name verbatim as a Lean identifier. The prose paraphrase supplies no letter ('finitely many real coefficients'), so the other branch chose 'a' and elaborates. The causal chain is airtight: the wording determined the name, and the name is a reserved token.
   ERROR [elaboration_failure] This branch does not elaborate against Mathlib rev 8f9d9cff6b on Lean 4.28.0: it does not parse. Lean reports: unexpected token 'λ'; expected '_' or identifier
     why  : A generation defect at the syntactic level.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: A third distinct mechanism, and the most direct causal chain of any compile failure in the corpus: the rigorous paraphrase prints the coefficients as '$\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\dots,\lambda_n$', this branch used the printed name verbatim as a Lean binder, and 'λ' is Lean 4's reserved notation for 'fun' — so the statement does not parse ('unexpected token λ; expected _ or identifier'). The prose paraphrase names no letter at all ('given finitely many real COEFFICIENTS'), so the other branch chose 'a' and elaborates cleanly. Unlike the scoped-notation cases this is not about which Lean construct answers a symbol; the paraphrase's choice of VARIABLE NAME was itself unusable as an identifier.

#92 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $a:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}$ be a sequence of integers indexed by the natural numbers $0,1,2,\dots$ such that $a_1=2$ and $a_{n+1}=\left\lfloor \frac{3a_n}{2}\right\rfloor$ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$ (including $n=0$); prove that the set of indices $n\in\mathbb{N}$ with $a_n$ odd is infinite and the set of indices $n\in\mathbb{N}$ with $a_n$ even is infinite.
   Lean       : theorem infinitely_many_odd_and_even_indices (a : ℕ → ℤ) (h₁ : a 1 = 2) (hrec : ∀ n : ℕ, a (n + 1) = ⌊((3 : ℚ) * (a n : ℚ)) / 2⌋) : Set.Infinite {n : ℕ | Odd (a n)} ∧ Set.Infinite {n : ℕ | Even (a n)} := by sorry
   ERROR [hypothesis_set] The hypothesis set is UNSATISFIABLE, so the theorem is vacuously true. `a 1 = 2` together with the recurrence instantiated at `n = 0` demands an integer `a 0` with `⌊3*a 0/2⌋ = 2`, and no such integer exists.
     why  : The map `t ↦ ⌊3t/2⌋` skips 2: `1 ↦ 1` and `2 ↦ 3`. Verified by exhaustive search over `a 0 ∈ [-1000, 1000]`, which returns the empty set (and the map is monotone, so the search is conclusive). Inherited from the reference `algebra_429555`, which has the same `∀ n` recurrence including `n = 0`.
     not attributed to the wording

#127 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Compute $\prod_{n=2}^{6} \frac{n^{3}-1}{n^{3}+1}$ as a real number. Prove that the answer is $\frac{43}{63}$.
   Lean       : theorem product_cubic_fraction : (∏ n ∈ Finset.Icc 2 6, (((n : ℝ) ^ 3 - 1) / ((n : ℝ) ^ 3 + 1))) = (43 : ℝ) / 63 := by sorry
   ERROR [other] Elaboration failure: `failed to synthesize instance of type class LocallyFiniteOrder ℝ`.
     why  : `Finset.Icc a b` needs a `LocallyFiniteOrder` instance on the index type. With the index type left as a metavariable and the body's only constraint being the coercion `(n : ℝ)`, the elaborator solves the index type as ℝ, and ℝ -- being densely ordered -- has no `LocallyFiniteOrder` instance. Verified in Lean against the pinned Mathlib (rev 8f9d9cff6b): branch A verbatim fails with exactly this instance error at the `Finset.Icc` occurrence, while a control consisting of branch A with ONLY the index type ascribed (`(Finset.Icc 2 6 : Finset ℕ)`) and the ℝ casts left untouched elaborates cleanly. The ℝ-versus-ℚ domain choice is therefore NOT the cause; the missing index ascription is.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: WORDING-LINKED, with an isolating control. The prose paraphrase names the index domain ('For each WHOLE NUMBER from two through six'), which is why B ascribed `(Finset.Icc 2 6 : Finset ℕ)` and survived elaboration; the print paraphrase gives only the bare `\prod_{n=2}^{6}`, naming no index type, so A omitted the ascription and the index type was solved as ℝ. This is the SECOND instance of the missing-type-ascription mechanism (with claude:847) and the better-evidenced of the two, since here the control isolates the ascription from every other difference.

     why  : A generation defect: the statement is not well-formed as written, usually because an unbounded quantifier appears where a decidable predicate is required.

#144 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Prove that there exists a function $D:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ such that $D(x)=0$ for every irrational $x$, $D(x)=1$ for every rational $x$, $D(x+c)=D(x)$ for all $x\in\mathbb{R}$ and every real $c>0$, and the set $\{c>0:\ D(x+c)=D(x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}\}$ has no least element.
   Lean       : theorem exists_dirichlet_function_with_no_least_positive_period : ∃ D : ℝ → ℝ, (∀ x : ℝ, Irrational x → D x = 0) ∧ (∀ x : ℝ, (∃ q : ℚ, x = (q : ℝ)) → D x = 1) ∧ (∀ x c : ℝ, 0 < c → D (x + c) = D x) ∧ ¬ ∃ c : ℝ, IsLeast {d : ℝ | 0 < d ∧ ∀ x : ℝ, D (x + d) = D x} c := by sorry
   ERROR [other] The statement is false: no such function exists. Its first two conditions pin the function to be the indicator of the rationals, and the third then requires every positive real to be a period of that indicator, which fails - taking the period to be the square root of two and the argument zero gives value zero at the shifted point against value one at the origin.
     why  : The indicator of the rationals is periodic exactly with rational periods. An irrational period would carry the origin, where the value is one, to an irrational point, where the value is zero. So the conjunction of the three conditions is unsatisfiable and the existential claim is false. Only the fourth condition, the absence of a smallest positive period, is a genuine property of the function - the positive rationals have no least element.
     not attributed to the wording
   ERROR [other] Consequence of the same defect: the fourth condition is stated about the set of all positive periods, which under the third condition would be all positive reals and would indeed have no least element - so the two conditions are not independently contradictory, but the third alone is unsatisfiable.
     why  : Recorded separately only to note that the statement's falsity comes from the periodicity quantifier and not from the minimality clause, which is the interesting half of the problem.
     not attributed to the wording

   paraphrase : Let $x,y$ be positive integers such that $x = 10\,(y \bmod 10) + \lfloor y/10 \rfloor$, and suppose there exists a nonnegative integer $n$ with $n^2 = x^2 - y^2$, where this subtraction is the truncated subtraction of nonnegative integers (equal to $0$ whenever $x \le y$); prove that $x = 65$ and $y = 56$.
   Lean       : theorem digit_reversal_difference_of_squares (x y : ℕ) (hx : 0 < x) (hy : 0 < y) (hxy : x = 10 * (y % 10) + y / 10) (hn : ∃ n : ℕ, n ^ 2 = x ^ 2 - y ^ 2) : x = 65 ∧ y = 56 := by sorry
   ERROR [variable_domain] The difference of squares is computed in the naturals, where subtraction truncates to zero, so every pair in which the first number does not exceed the second satisfies the perfect-square condition vacuously - zero is a square. The digit-reversal condition does not exclude such pairs, so the conclusion is false. The smallest counterexample is the pair one and ten: the reversal condition holds, the truncated difference is zero, and the conclusion fails.
     why  : Exhaustive search over second numbers below four hundred finds three hundred fifty-four pairs satisfying both hypotheses, of which exactly one is the intended sixty-five and fifty-six; all the others have the first number at most the second, so their truncated difference is zero and the square witness is zero. Examples include twelve and twenty-one, twenty-three and thirty-two, and every pair of equal repdigits.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: The rigorous paraphrase does not merely inherit the artefact, it states it as part of the problem: 'where this subtraction is the truncated subtraction of nonnegative integers (equal to $0$ whenever $x \le y$)'. The formalization is a faithful rendering of that sentence. The defect originates in the reference, which performs the subtraction on naturals, and the paraphrase step turned an encoding artefact into a stipulated convention.

   paraphrase : Let $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$ satisfy $56x+33y=\dfrac{-y}{x^2+y^2}$ and $33x-56y=\dfrac{x}{x^2+y^2}$; determine $|x|+|y|$. Prove that the answer is $\frac{11}{65}$.
   Lean       : theorem determine_abs_sum (x y : ℝ) (h₁ : 56 * x + 33 * y = -y / (x ^ 2 + y ^ 2)) (h₂ : 33 * x - 56 * y = x / (x ^ 2 + y ^ 2)) : |x| + |y| = (11 : ℝ) / 65 := by sorry
   ERROR [hypothesis_set] The statement is FALSE. `(x, y) = (0, 0)` satisfies both hypotheses but not the conclusion.
     why  : In Lean division by zero returns zero, so at `x = y = 0` the right-hand sides are `-0 / 0 = 0` and `0 / 0 = 0`, while the left-hand sides are `56*0 + 33*0 = 0` and `33*0 - 56*0 = 0`. This is the corpus's clearest instance of a paraphrase difference flipping the TRUTH VALUE of a formalization through Lean's division-by-zero convention -- not a compile failure, but a genuine soundness difference.

     why  : At a = b = c = 1, a' = b' = c' = 1 and A = B = C = pi/3 every hypothesis is satisfied (the law of sines and all three laws of cosines hold, the angles lie in (0,pi) and sum to pi), and the left side is 3 * tan(pi/6) = sqrt 3 = 1.7320508 while the right side is sqrt 3 * 3 / (2 * 3) = sqrt 3 / 2 = 0.8660254 - a factor of two apart. So the equiangular configuration does not give equality, and the 'only if' half of the biconditional fails outright. A random search over 200000 valid configurations never brought the ratio below 2.0019, so equality is in fact unattainable and the inequality, while true, is off by a factor of two. The intended statement takes s' to be the semiperimeter: replacing 2*(a'+b'+c') by a'+b'+c' makes the minimum ratio 1.0016 over 80000 configurations, attained near A = B = C = A' = B' = C' = pi/3 - exactly the claimed equality case. The defect is upstream: the reference itself carries hs' : s' = a' + b' + c', both paraphrases copied that definition faithfully, and all four formalizations of this problem across both models inherit it.
     not attributed to the wording

#358 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $x,y,z\ge 0$ be real numbers; prove that $x(y+z-x)^2+y(z+x-y)^2+z(x+y-z)^2\ge 3xyz$, with equality if and only if $x=y=z$.

   ERROR [other] The 'equality if and only if' half of the conclusion is false: equality also holds whenever one variable is zero and the other two are equal.
     why  : Take x = y = 1 and z = 0. All three nonnegativity hypotheses hold. Each summand vanishes - x(y+z-x)^2 = 1*(1+0-1)^2 = 0, y(z+x-y)^2 = 1*(0+1-1)^2 = 0, and z(x+y-z)^2 = 0 - so the left side is 0, and the right side is 3*1*1*0 = 0. Equality therefore holds while x = y = z fails, refuting the reverse direction of the biconditional. A grid search over [0,3]^3 in steps of 1/4 finds 36 such equality points, all of the form (0,t,t) and its permutations, so there are infinitely many counterexamples. The inequality itself is true (the grid search finds no violation); only the equality characterisation is wrong, and it is wrong in the reference too, which both paraphrases copied faithfully.
     not attributed to the wording

#428 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $A_1,A_2,A_3$ be three non-collinear points in the Euclidean plane, let $P$ be an arbitrary point of that plane, let $a_i$ be the distance from $A_i$ to $A_{i+1}$ (indices taken cyclically modulo $3$), let $R_i$ be the distance from $P$ to $A_i$ for $i=1,2,3$, and let $\Delta$ be the area of triangle $A_1A_2A_3$; prove that $(a_2^2+a_3^2-a_1^2)R_1^2+(a_3^2+a_1^2-a_2^2)R_2^2+(a_1^2+a_2^2-a_3^2)
   Lean       : theorem triangle_distance_inequality (A₁ A₂ A₃ P : EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)) (a₁ a₂ a₃ R₁ R₂ R₃ Δ : ℝ) (hnc : (A₂ 0 - A₁ 0) * (A₃ 1 - A₁ 1) - (A₂ 1 - A₁ 1) * (A₃ 0 - A₁ 0) ≠ 0) (ha₁ : a₁ = dist A₁ A₂) (ha₂ : a₂ = dist A₂ A₃) (ha₃ : a₃ = dist A₃ A₁) (hR₁ : R₁ = dist P A₁) (hR₂ : R₂ = dist P A₂) (hR₃ : R₃ = dist P A₃) (hΔ : Δ = abs ((A₂ 0 - A₁ 0) * (A₃ 1 - A₁ 1) - (A₂ 1 - A₁ 1) * (A₃ 0 - A₁ 0)) / 2)
   ERROR [other] The inequality is false as stated. The weight attached to the squared distance from P to a vertex is the sum of the squares of the other two sides minus the square of that vertex's own assigned side, where the assigned side is one of the two sides through the vertex. The correct weighting - the one that makes this a true theorem - subtracts the square of the side opposite the vertex, giving twice the product of the two adjacent sides with the cosine of the angle.
     why  : Exact counterexample, all rational: take the vertices (0,0), (1,0), (2,1/10), which are not collinear since the cross product is 1/10, and take P to be the first vertex. Then the three squared side lengths are 1, 101/100 and 401/100, so the three weights are 201/50, 4 and -2, and the two nonzero distance terms give 4*1 + (-2)*(401/100) = -201/50 = -4.02. The right-hand side is 16/3 times the square of the area 1/20, that is 1/75. So the claimed inequality reads -4.02 >= 0.0133. The failure is systematic rather than accidental: the total of the three weights is the sum of the squares of the sides and hence positive, so the left side is a convex quadratic in P whose minimum I computed in closed form; under the stated weighting that minimum falls below the right-hand side for every sufficiently flat triangle, with a worst slack of about -323 over 400000 random non-degenerate triangles, whereas under the correct opposite-side weighting the minimum slack over the same sample is zero to six decimals. Both weightings agree at the equilateral triangle, where all three sides are equal and the slack is exactly zero at the centroid, which is presumably why the mis-indexing survived: it is invisible at the equality case.
     not attributed to the wording
   ERROR [elaboration_failure] This branch does not elaborate against Mathlib rev 8f9d9cff6b on Lean 4.28.0: it emits a preamble whose 'open' line names a namespace that does not exist. Lean reports: unknown namespace `IntervalIntegral`
     why  : The statement body may be fine; the failure comes from the model's own preamble, so it is a generation defect but of a different kind from a defective statement.

#476 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $k$ be a positive integer. Show that the minimum value of $x^{k+1}+y^{k+1}+z^{k+1}$ over all real numbers $x,y,z$ satisfying $x^{k}+y^{k}+z^{k}=1$ is attained and equals $3^{-\frac{1}{k}}$; that is, prove that the answer is $3^{-\frac{1}{k}}$.
   Lean       : theorem minimum_power_sum (k : ℕ) (hk : 0 < k) : ∃ x y z : ℝ, x ^ k + y ^ k + z ^ k = 1 ∧ x ^ (k + 1) + y ^ (k + 1) + z ^ (k + 1) = Real.rpow (3 : ℝ) (-(1 : ℝ) / (k : ℝ)) ∧ ∀ a b c : ℝ, a ^ k + b ^ k + c ^ k = 1 → Real.rpow (3 : ℝ) (-(1 : ℝ) / (k : ℝ)) ≤ a ^ (k + 1) + b ^ (k + 1) + c ^ (k + 1) := by sorry
   ERROR [variable_domain] The claimed minimum is wrong whenever k is even, because the constraint is imposed on real numbers of either sign and an odd power of a negative number is negative. The intended problem is about positive reals.
     why  : Explicit counterexample for every even k: the triple (-1, 0, 0) satisfies the constraint, since the k-th powers sum to 1 when k is even, and its sum of (k+1)-st powers is -1, which is below the claimed minimum of 3 to the power minus one over k - about 0.577 for k = 2 and 0.760 for k = 4. So the lower-bound half of the minimality claim fails outright. A numerical search over 400000 random triples confirms the picture: for k = 1, 3 and 5 the numeric minimum agrees with the formula to five decimals and is attained on the diagonal, while for k = 2 and k = 4 it is -1.000000. Restricting to positive reals makes the formula correct, by the power mean inequality - the diagonal triple has each entry equal to 3 to the power minus one over k, and the sum of its (k+1)-st powers is then exactly 3 to the power minus one over k.
     not attributed to the wording

   paraphrase : Let $\alpha \in (0,\pi)$ and let $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ satisfy $x_1^2 - x_1\sin\alpha + \cos\alpha = 0$ and $x_2^2 - x_2\sin\alpha + \cos\alpha = 0$; prove that $\arctan x_1 + \arctan x_2 = \frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\alpha}{2}$ or $\arctan x_1 + \arctan x_2 = -\frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\alpha}{2}$.
   Lean       : theorem arctan_sum_of_quadratic_roots (α x₁ x₂ : ℝ) (hα : 0 < α ∧ α < Real.pi) (hx₁ : x₁ ^ 2 - x₁ * Real.sin α + Real.cos α = 0) (hx₂ : x₂ ^ 2 - x₂ * Real.sin α + Real.cos α = 0) : Real.arctan x₁ + Real.arctan x₂ = Real.pi / 2 - α / 2 ∨ Real.arctan x₁ + Real.arctan x₂ = -Real.pi / 2 - α / 2 := by sorry
   ERROR [hypothesis_set] The statement is false because it only requires each of the two numbers to be a root, which permits them to be the same root. The intended hypothesis is that they are the two roots of the quadratic.
     why  : Explicit counterexample: take the angle to be a right angle, so the sine is one and the cosine is zero and the quadratic is the variable squared minus the variable, with roots zero and one. Setting both numbers to one satisfies both hypotheses, and the sum of their arctangents is twice a quarter turn, that is pi over two, about 1.570796. The two permitted answers are a right angle minus half the angle, which is pi over four or about 0.785398, and its counterpart, about -2.356194. Neither matches. Setting both numbers to zero instead gives a sum of zero, which also matches neither. By contrast taking the two distinct roots zero and one gives exactly pi over four, so the intended statement is fine - the identity is that the tangent of the sum of the arctangents is the sum of the roots over one minus their product, which is the sine over one minus the cosine, the cotangent of half the angle. That argument needs the two numbers to be the two distinct roots, and nothing in the statement says so.
     not attributed to the wording

#593 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Let $f \in \mathbb{Z}[x]$ be a polynomial with $\deg f > 0$. Prove that there exists $n \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that the number of distinct primes $p$ with $p \mid f(n)$ is at least $2004$.
   Lean       : theorem polynomial_value_has_many_prime_divisors (f : Polynomial ℤ) (hf : 0 < f.degree) : ∃ n : ℤ, ∃ S : Finset ℕ, 2004 ≤ S.card ∧ ∀ p ∈ S, Nat.Prime p ∧ (p : ℤ) ∣ f.eval n := by sorry
   ERROR [other] The statement is VACUOUSLY SATISFIABLE for any polynomial having an integer root.
     why  : The existential `∃ n, ∃ S : Finset ℕ, 2004 ≤ S.card ∧ ∀ p ∈ S, Nat.Prime p ∧ (p : ℤ) ∣ f.eval n` is trivially witnessed by an integer root `n` (where `f.eval n = 0`) together with any 2004 primes, since every prime divides 0. The theorem therefore has no content for such polynomials, whereas the intended claim -- and the reference's `Nat.primeFactors` formulation -- does.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: CAUSED BY THE PARAPHRASE, in the negative sense: the printed paraphrase phrases the count as a DIVISIBILITY count ('the number of distinct primes $p$ with $p \mid f(n)$'), which is precisely the formulation that degenerates at zero, and this branch reproduced it without a nonvacuity guard. The prose paraphrase says the value 'HAS ... distinct prime factors', whose presupposition of nonzero-ness the other branch encoded as `f.eval n ≠ 0`. So the two paraphrases are mathematically equivalent as English but only one of them carries the cue that prevents the degenerate reading.

   Lean       : theorem sequence_difference_bound (a : ℕ → ℝ) (h_nonneg : ∀ k : ℕ, 0 ≤ a k) (h_convex : ∀ k : ℕ, 0 ≤ a k - 2 * a (k + 1) + a (k + 2)) (h_sum : ∀ k : ℕ, (∑ j ∈ Finset.Icc 1 k, a j) ≤ 1) : ∀ k : ℕ, 0 ≤ a k - a (k + 1) ∧ a k - a (k + 1) < if k = 0 then 0 else 2 / (k : ℝ) ^ 2 := by sorry
   ERROR [hypothesis_set] The conclusion is self-contradictory at index zero, so the statement is false: it asserts both that the difference of the first two terms is nonnegative and that it is strictly less than zero.
     why  : At index zero the bound is zero, whether written as an explicit conditional or obtained from Lean's division-by-zero convention, so the conclusion demands a quantity that is at the same time at least zero and strictly below zero - impossible. The hypotheses are satisfiable, which is what makes this a refutation rather than a vacuity: the identically zero sequence is nonnegative, satisfies the convexity condition as an equality, and has all partial sums zero, hence at most one. So all three hypotheses hold and the conclusion fails at index zero. The intended problem plainly quantifies over indices from one onward.
     not attributed to the wording

The exponent $-Q(t)$ has an interior maximum at $t^* = -0.5679051$ with $-Q(t^*) = 0.2575822$ and $Q''(t^*) = 4.87032$, so Laplace's method gives $I(x) \sim 0.4515284\,e^{0.2575822x}/\sqrt{x}$. #462 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Prove that there exists $\theta \in \left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ such that $z^{2}+8z+9=(z-\tan\theta)(z-\tan 3\theta)$ for every complex number $z$; that is, show that the answer is yes.
   Lean       : theorem exists_theta_tan_factorization : ∃ θ : ℝ, θ ∈ Set.Ioo (-(Real.pi / 2)) (Real.pi / 2) ∧ ∀ z : ℂ, z ^ 2 + 8 * z + 9 = (z - (Real.tan θ : ℂ)) * (z - (Real.tan (3 * θ) : ℂ)) := by sorry
   ERROR [other] The statement is FALSE. Factoring $z^2+8z+9$ as $(z-\tan\theta)(z-\tan 3\theta)$ forces $\tan\theta + \tan 3\theta = -8$ AND $\tan\theta \cdot \tan 3\theta = 9$, and no real $\theta$ satisfies both.
     why  : With $t = \tan\theta$ and the triple-angle identity, the sum condition reduces to the cubic $t^3+6t^2-t-2=0$ and the product condition to the quartic $t^4-30t^2+9=0$ (roots $t = \pm(3\pm\sqrt6)$); the two share no root. Verified numerically: the minimum of $|\tan\theta+\tan3\theta+8| + |\tan\theta\tan3\theta-9|$ over 2 million grid points of $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$ is $0.899$, bounded well away from zero. Equivalently, the polynomial's roots are $-4\pm\sqrt7$, and $\tan\theta = -1.3542$ gives $\tan3\theta = 0.3508$ while $\tan\theta = -6.6458$ gives $\tan3\theta = -2.0805$ -- neither is the other root.
     not attributed to the wording

#521 [rigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Find the sum of all positive integers $n \leq 2015$ for which there exist positive integers $x$ and $y$ with $n = \left\lceil \frac{x}{2} \right\rceil + y + xy$. Prove that the answer is $2029906$.
   Lean       : theorem sum_of_valid_n : (∑ n ∈ (Finset.Icc 1 2015).filter (fun n : ℕ => ∃ x y : ℕ, 0 < x ∧ 0 < y ∧ (n : ℤ) = ⌈(x : ℚ) / 2⌉ + (y : ℤ) + (x : ℤ) * (y : ℤ)), n) = 2029906 := by sorry
   ERROR [other] The stated answer 2029906 is WRONG; the correct sum is 2030981. Both branches (and the reference) assert the false value.
     why  : Characterise the representable $n$: for $x = 2k-1$ odd, $n = k(2y+1)$, so $n$ is representable iff it has an odd divisor $\ge 3$, i.e. iff $n$ is not a power of 2; for $x = 2k$ even, $2n+1 = (2k+1)(2y+1)$, so $n$ is representable iff $2n+1$ is composite. Hence $n \le 2015$ is NOT representable exactly when $n$ is a power of 2 AND $2n+1$ is prime, which happens only for $n \in \{1, 2, 8, 128\}$ (since $2\cdot4+1=9$, $2\cdot16+1=33$, $2\cdot32+1=65$, $2\cdot64+1=129$, $2\cdot256+1=513$, $2\cdot512+1=1025$ and $2\cdot1024+1=2049$ are all composite). So the answer is $\frac{2015\cdot2016}{2} - (1+2+8+128) = 2031120 - 139 = 2030981$. Confirmed independently by direct enumeration over all $x \le 4200$, $y \le 2100$, which yields 2011 representable values summing to 2030981.
     not attributed to the wording

     why  : The inner intervalIntegral ∫ ρ in (0:ℝ)..(a * sin (2φ)), ρ² is the SIGNED integral, equal to (a sin 2φ)³/3 for every φ including those where sin 2φ < 0. Numerically the outer integral is 0 (2.4e-38 at 30-digit precision) for a = 1, while (8/9)a³ = 0.8889. The intended value 8a³/9 is the integral of |a sin 2φ|³/3 over φ ∈ [0, 2π], i.e. over the full four-petal rose ρ = a sin 2φ; with signed limits on [0, π] the petals cancel.    ERROR [other] The statement is FALSE, inherited from the reference: it fails at p = 3, the smallest odd prime allowed by the hypotheses.
     why  : With p = 3, 3n = 2^6 - 1 = 63 so n = 21, and the hypotheses (p prime, p odd, 3n = 2^{2p} - 1) are all satisfied. #479 [nonrigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: NOT_EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Suppose two finite collections of nonnegative whole numbers each contain at least two members, and suppose a further finite collection of nonnegative whole numbers has exactly one fewer member than the two collections have in total, each of its members being the product of a member of the first collection with a member of the second; prove that this last collection contains three distinct members,
   Lean       : theorem exists_three_with_dvd (m n : ℕ) (hm : 2 ≤ m) (hn : 2 ≤ n) (a : Fin m → ℕ) (b : Fin n → ℕ) (c : Fin (m + n - 1) → ℕ) (hc : ∀ k : Fin (m + n - 1), ∃ (i : Fin m) (j : Fin n), c k = a i * b j) : ∃ k₁ k₂ k₃ : Fin (m + n - 1), k₁ ≠ k₂ ∧ k₁ ≠ k₃ ∧ k₂ ≠ k₃ ∧ c k₁ ∣ c k₂ * c k₃ := by sorry
   ERROR [statement_structure] B's indexed-family encoding loses distinctness in two places at once: the third collection is no longer required to consist of distinct numbers, and the conclusion only demands three distinct indices rather than three distinct members.
     why  : The problem is about sets, where |S| = |A| + |B| - 1 counts distinct products - that is the whole force of the hypothesis. An arbitrary c : Fin (m + n - 1) -> N may repeat values, so B's hypothesis is far weaker than the stated one. Worse, the conclusion becomes nearly vacuous: with repeated values one can take k1, k2, k3 distinct as indices while c k1 = c k2 = c k3, and divisibility is then automatic. B therefore states a much weaker theorem than the paraphrase.
     WORDING-DRIVEN: 'collections' and 'members' with sizes given as counts ('each contain at least two members', 'exactly one fewer member than the two collections have in total') rather than as cardinality bars. Replacing $|A|$, $|B|$, $|S|$ with counted members is what invited the switch from Finset to Fin-indexed family, and set semantics went with it.

   paraphrase : Taking a real variable, consider the fraction whose numerator is two more than the variable and whose denominator is the square root of the quadratic given by six plus ten times the variable plus twenty-five times the square of the variable, and prove that an antiderivative of this fraction is one twenty-fifth of that same square root, plus nine twenty-fifths of the natural logarithm of the sum of
   Lean       : theorem antiderivative_of_fraction (C : ℝ) (f : ℝ → ℝ) (hf : ∀ x : ℝ, f x = (1 / 25) * Real.sqrt (6 + 10 * x + 25 * x ^ 2) + (9 / 25) * Real.log (1 + 5 * x + Real.sqrt (1 + (1 + 5 * x) ^ 2)) + C) : ∀ x : ℝ, HasDerivAt f ((x + 2) / Real.sqrt (6 + 10 * x + 25 * x ^ 2)) x := by sorry

#871 [nonrigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : For every real number $x$, let $I(x)$ denote the integral, taken as $t$ runs from minus six tenths up to minus two tenths, of the product of two quantities: the first is two and one tenth times the fifth power of $t$ plus the sine of $t$, and the second is the exponential of $x$ times the quantity obtained by subtracting one and one tenth times the square of $t$ from one and one half times the cos
   Lean       : theorem laplace_asymptotic (I : ℝ → ℝ) (hI : ∀ x : ℝ, I x = ∫ t in (-0.6 : ℝ)..(-0.2 : ℝ), (2.1 * t ^ 5 + Real.sin t) * Real.exp (x * (1.5 * Real.cos t - 1.1 * t ^ 2))) : Filter.Tendsto (fun x : ℝ => I x / (-0.27 * Real.exp (1.43 * x) / x)) Filter.atTop (nhds 1) := by sorry

#880 [nonrigorous]  (stage-3 verdict: EQUIVALENT)
   paraphrase : Suppose three prime numbers and a natural number are such that the sum of the three pairwise products of the primes equals one more than twelve times the natural number; show that the answer is that the natural number is five and the three primes are two, three and eleven in some order.
   Lean       : theorem primes_pairwise_products (p q r n : ℕ) (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hr : r.Prime) (h : p * q + q * r + r * p = 12 * n + 1) : n = 5 ∧ ({p, q, r} : Multiset ℕ) = {2, 3, 11} := by sorry
